Nevada Power Company d/b/a NV Energy (“Nevada Power”) and Sierra Pacific Power

9

Company d/b/a NV Energy (“Sierra” and, together with Nevada Power, the “Companies”) make

10

this joint Application, pursuant to Nevada Revised Statute (“NRS”) § 704.741 et seq. and Nevada

11

Administrative Code (“NAC”) § 704.9005 et seq. The Joint Application requests approval by the

12

Public Utilities Commission of Nevada (“Commission”) of the Companies’ 2027-2046 joint

13

triennial integrated resource plan (“2026 Joint IRP”), including the plan of action (“Action Plan”)

14

and energy supply plan for the three-year period 2027-2029 (“2026 ESP”). NRS § 704.751(1)

15

requires that a triennial IRP must be processed within 210 days and an ESP within 135 days.

16

I.

17

SUMMARY AND INTRODUCTION

18

NV Energy makes this 2026 Joint IRP filing at a pivotal time. According to the North

19

American Electric Reliability Corporation (“NERC”), the industry is “entering a period of load

20

growth unprecedented in recent memory, driven by data centers [(e.g., artificial intelligence and

21

computational load)] that support” daily life and “advance North America’s leadership in the

22

deployment of artificial intelligence.” 1 Nevada is experiencing this same data-center-driven load

23

growth dynamic.

24
25
26

Correspondence from NERC, dated March 20, 2026, to the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (“FERC”),
available
at
https://www.nerc.com/globalassets/who-we-are/legal--regulatory/filings--orders/nerc-filings-toferc/2026/nerc_accelerated-ll-action-plan_rm26-4_signed.pdf.
1

27
28

1
Page 3 of 301

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

1

Nevada—and northern Nevada in particular—is a sought-after location for data centers due

2

to the expanse of developable land, a favorable climate for efficient cooling, and robust fiber

3

connectivity. NV Energy has received approximately 22,000 megawatts (“MW”) or 22 gigawatts

4

(“GW”) of interest inquiries from data centers and has executed approximately 6 GW of signed

5

Rule 9 agreements (generally consisting of master planned community (“MPC”) umbrella

6

agreements, substation high voltage distribution (“SHVD”) agreements, and high voltage

7

distribution (“HVD”) agreements) to facilitate the transmission, substation, and high voltage

8

distribution infrastructure needed to interconnect these large loads. These agreements do not

9

address generation and electric supply. As interconnecting load is typically heavily discounted in

10

IRP load forecasts, the Companies must plan prudently for the potential that these loads materialize.

11

This planning is particularly critical given that approximately 84 percent (almost 5 GW) of the

12

signed agreements are with large-load customers in northern Nevada—Sierra’s load forecast

13

projects kilowatt-hour (“kWh”) sales will double by 2029. Putting it in perspective, Nevada

14

Power’s peak load for 2025 was 6,168 MW and Sierra’s peak load for 2025 was 2,073 MW.

15

Projected load growth represents a step-change with unique risks and opportunities. On the

16

risk side of the scale, growth is concentrated in large loads, with individual load interconnection

17

requests commonly in the hundreds of MW. Utilities responding to this step-change in load need to

18

make significant investment in generation and transmission infrastructure to meet rapidly evolving

19

energy needs. On the opportunity side of the scale, large load additions have exhibited high load

20

factors. Stated differently, data centers have historically used energy consistently from hour-to-

21

hour, with minimal variation between low-load and high-load hours. This “flatter” load improves

22

system utilization, potentially allowing fixed costs to be spread across more sales.

23

NV Energy responded to these planning challenges through a deliberate, risk-aware

24

planning framework anchored by the Large Load Electric Service Agreement (“LLESA”) and a

25

flexibility-focused Preferred Plan. The Companies employ resource planning analysis for both a

26

Base Load Forecast and a reduced Base Minus Load Forecast to isolate the incremental generation

27

and transmission needs—and associated costs—that NV Energy would not incur but for large-load

28

2
Page 4 of 301

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

1

service requests. The LLESA contains several risk mitigation provisions designed to ensure that

2

large load additions pay for the infrastructure needed to meet their service requests. These include

3

customer specific charges that operate as contributions in aid of construction to reduce the

4

Companies’ investment in generation and transmission additions, minimum demand and energy

5

charges, security requirements, and termination payments. This approach, coupled with similar

6

provisions in Rule 9 agreements, provides a transparent, fact-based foundation for aligning cost

7

responsibility with cost causation and ensures that households, small businesses, and existing

8

commercial and industrial customers are not asked to bear costs driven primarily by new large-load

9

service.

10

In parallel, the Companies’ Preferred Plan—Obsidian—reflects a deliberate emphasis on

11

flexibility and executability. NV Energy developed the renewable energy expansion plan, which

12

seeks approval of 23 third-party power purchase agreements (“PPAs”) adding approximately 4,550

13

MW of renewable generation and 5,405 MW of BESS output capacity across the Nevada Power

14

and Sierra systems to support Renewable Portfolio Standard (“RPS”) compliance, load growth,

15

and reliability needs. The plan was developed through the 2024 All-Source Request for Proposals

16

(“RFP”) using selection criteria recalibrated to emphasize deliverability and executability—

17

prioritizing projects with lower federal permitting exposure, stronger tax-credit “safe harbor”

18

positioning, and near-term commercial online date (“COD”) potential—while targeting resources

19

that can help relieve emerging energy and capacity constraints in northern Nevada (including

20

Sierra’s import-constrained system).

21

Likewise, the thermal additions in the Obsidian Plan include 802 MW of aeroderivative

22

peaking turbines (centered around 2030–2031) and 421 MW of frame peaking turbines in the

23

early-2030s, providing fast-start, flexible capacity to support resource adequacy. They were

24

selected because they minimize execution and supply-chain risk—particularly by avoiding heavy

25

reliance on multiple frame combustion turbines with 2031 commercial operation dates—while also

26

limiting near-term capital exposure and preserving planning flexibility amid large-load

27
28

3
Page 5 of 301

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

1

uncertainty. This design directly addresses uncertainty around the timing, magnitude, and

2

geographic concentration of large-load growth, while preserving optionality.

3

Taken together, the LLESA approach, the load-scenario framework, and the flexible

4

resource portfolio translate emerging national reliability guidance and empirical load trends into

5

concrete planning and contracting tools. This integrated approach reduces stranded-asset risk,

6

supports timely and reliable service to new customers, and protects affordability and reliability for

7

existing customers as Nevada’s future load trajectory takes shape.

8

In conclusion, NV Energy’s policy position is clear: the Companies plan to support data

9

center development in Nevada, but only with prudent guardrails that protect reliability and ensure

10

that costs caused by large-load additions are borne by those loads, not shifted to existing customers.

11

Accordingly, NV Energy’s planning and service framework prioritizes deliverable, flexible

12

resources and infrastructure that can be timed and scaled to actual load realization, while

13

preserving optionality under evolving federal policy, permitting, and supply-chain conditions.

14

Consistent with these principles, a primary objective of this 2026 Joint IRP filing is to establish

15

clear, durable planning and service rules to address step-change load growth that are just,

16

reasonable, and in the public interest.

17

The Companies seek approval of the Preferred Plan resources; however, the need for these

18

resources is premised on the Base Load Forecast and the large new loads that underpin it. By

19

expeditiously reviewing and approving the LLESA framework, the Commission will give much

20

needed certainty to the Companies and the development community around the cost responsibility

21

for the new generation and transmission needed to serve the large new loads. This certainty, in

22

turn, would allow the Companies to firm up the load forecast and align with the necessary

23

resources needed to support the approved load forecast.

24

NRS § 704.751(1)(a) requires that the Commission issue an order accepting or modifying

25

the IRP or specifying any portions of the Joint Application it deems to be inadequate, within 210

26

days after its filing. The statutory period within which this matter must be resolved, therefore, runs

27

on Wednesdays, December 2, 2026. To gain certainty around the LLESA framework, the

28

4
Page 6 of 301

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

1

Companies request an expedited ruling on the form LLESA. 2 The Commission ruling will allow

2

large load customers the opportunity to determine if they want to move forward with execution of

3

their respective LLESAs, which will have an impact on the resources needed to serve such load.

4

Accordingly, the Companies request that the form contract approval of the LLESA proceed on the

5

same timeline as the concurrently filed ESP, which would set a deemed approved date of Friday,

6

September 18, 2026.

7

II.

8

THE APPLICANTS

9

Nevada Power and Sierra are Nevada corporations and wholly owned subsidiaries of NV

10

Energy, Inc. Nevada Power and Sierra are public utilities as defined in NRS § 704.020 and are

11

subject to the jurisdiction of the Commission. Nevada Power is engaged in providing electric

12

service to the public in portions of Clark and Nye counties, Nevada pursuant to a certificate of

13

public convenience and necessity issued by this Commission. Sierra provides electric service to

14

the public in portions of fourteen northern Nevada counties, including the communities of Carson

15

City, Minden, Gardnerville, Reno, Sparks, and Elko. Sierra owns and operates a certificated local

16

distribution company engaged in the retail sale of natural gas to customers in the Reno-Sparks

17

metropolitan area.

18

Sierra’s primary business office is located at 6100 Neil Road in Reno, Nevada, and Nevada

19

Power’s primary business office is located at 6226 West Sahara Avenue in Las Vegas, Nevada.

ADDITIONAL SUPPORTING MATERIAL

3

Section 704.9321 of the NAC provides that a utility’s resource plan must be based on

4

substantially accurate data, adequately demonstrated and defended, and adequately documented

5

and defended. As is set forth below, included in this 2026 Joint IRP, and incorporated herein by

6

reference, the reader will find all material required to adequately demonstrate and defend the

7

substantially accurate data supporting the analysis and the requests for affirmative relief set forth

8

herein. Shawn Elicegui, Senior Vice President, Regulatory and Resource Planning, provides

9

support for the overall policy of the 2026 IRP. Janet Wells, Vice President, Integrated Resource

10

Planning, identifies the sponsors of each of the substantive portions of the 2026 Joint IRP. A

11

summary of this information, which includes narrative, technical appendices, 3 and supporting

12

prepared direct testimony, 4 is set forth by general topic below.

NAC § 704.922 requires that a utility’s resource plan include technical appendices that contain sufficient detail to
enable a technically proficient reader to understand how the resource plan and its forecasts were prepared and to
evaluate the validity of the assumptions and the accuracy of the data used, including, without limitation, a list of the
major assumptions used, a description of the forecasting methods employed and a description of the software utilized.

14

15

16

17

18

energy and capacity in the wholesale market. In seeking or responding to requests for proposals

19

(“RFPs”), the confidentiality of the Companies’ price forecasts is key to the competitive process.

20

Therefore, it is fundamentally contrary to the interests of customers to provide public access to

21

Companies’ confidential price forecasts for market energy and fuels.

VI.

13

REQUEST FOR DEVIATION

14
15

NAC § 704.0097 provides that the Commission may allow deviation from any
provision of NAC Chapter 704 if:

16

(1) Good cause for the deviation appears;

17

(2) The person requesting the deviation provides a specific reference to each provision of

18
19
20

the chapter from which the deviation is requested; and
(3) The Commission finds that the deviation is in the public interest and is not contrary to
statute.

21

NAC § 704.9237(2)(f) states that the DRP must:

22

Be developed by a utility using a forecast of net distribution system load and
distributed resources. The forecast must be for a period of not less than 6 years,
beginning with the year after the distributed resources plan is filed. The net
distribution system load and distributed resources forecast will include system,
substation and feeder level net load projections and energy and demand
characteristics for all distributed resource types.

23
24
25
26

The Companies utilized net distribution feeder, substation transformer, and transmission

27

forecasts to determine the constraints on the transmission and distribution systems. System-level

28

25
Page 27 of 301

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

1

forecasts for certain distributed resource types are filed with the Commission in the Load Forecast

2

and Technical Appendix LF-1 of this 2024 Joint IRP filing and the DRP does not alter those

3

forecasts in any way. However, all distributed resource types as defined in NAC § 704.90583 are

4

not yet represented in those forecasts. Also of note is that the Companies have disaggregated

5

system-level private solar photovoltaic (“PV”), energy efficiency, and demand response forecasts

6

down to the substation and feeder levels, as noted in the DRP. As a result, NV Energy requests a

7

waiver of NAC § 704.9237(2)(f) as it continues to investigate alternative methods for developing

8

substation and feeder level DER forecasts. The Companies’ request to deviate is in the public

9

interest because it does not impact the analysis or conclusion of the DRP and recognizes the DRP

10

is an evolving process that requires some flexibility.

11

In addition, NAC § 704.9237(3)(b) states that the DRP must include a hosting capacity

12

analysis of the distribution system evaluated “under normal conditions and planned and unplanned

13

contingency conditions.” The Companies completed the hosting capacity analysis (“HCA”) for the

14

DRP under normal system operating conditions, but not under planned or unplanned contingency

15

conditions. The Companies discussed the issue of performing HCA under contingency conditions

16

internally and initially concluded that any contingencies analyzed would need to be limited in

17

scope to ensure that the number of cases analyzed do not create an untenable situation in terms of

18

being able to complete the analysis. After investigation with stakeholders, the Companies

19

identified no other jurisdictions or utilities that perform HCA under contingency conditions and

20

no generally known or accepted industry technique for doing so. NV Energy will continue to

21

monitor this issue to ascertain whether there are any techniques that can be applied to ensure that

22

the analytical process would not be made impractical by the addition of contingency conditions.

23

The Companies request a waiver from NAC § 704.9237(3)(b). The Companies’ request to deviate

24

is in the public interest because it does not impact the analysis or conclusion of the DRP and

25

recognizes the DRP is an evolving process that requires flexibility.

26
27
28

26

ACTION PLAN
Nevada Power Company d/b/a NV Energy
Sierra Pacific Power Company d/b/a NV Energy
2026 Joint Integrated Resource Plan
Docket No. 26-05___
Action Plan Period January 1, 2027, to December 31, 2029
SECTION I
INTRODUCTION — NAC § 704.9489(1)(a)
Nevada Power Company (“Nevada Power”) and Sierra Pacific Power Company (“Sierra” and,
together with Nevada Power, the “Companies” or “NV Energy”) are filing this joint integrated
resource plan (“2026 Joint IRP”). The 2026 Joint IRP focuses on deliverability, reliability,
flexibility, and affordability. The 2026 Joint IRP continues the Companies’ commitment to
meeting the energy needs and demands of their customers while also striving to meet the state’s
clean energy policies and goals. In determining their Preferred Plan and preparing the Action Plan,
the Companies developed five long-term primary expansion plans for meeting customers’
demands and tested them to determine how each performed across the range of potential load, fuel
and purchased power prices, and federal climate policy scenarios. The Companies have selected
as their Preferred Plan the Obsidian Plan, the centerpiece of which is:
•

4,370 MW of new paired solar PV PPA projects by 2031 as presented in the
Renewables Section of the Supply Plan

•

5,405 MW of new paired and standalone BESS PPA projects by 2031 as presented in
the Renewables Section of the Supply Plan

•

180 MW of new geothermal PPA projects by 2031 as presented in the Renewables
Section of the Supply Plan

•

The following new thermal resources as presented in the Generation Section of the
Supply Plan, stated with nominal ratings:
a) Fort Churchill Additions 1 Project – 401 MW new simple-cycle aeroderivative gas
turbines at the Fort Churchill Generation Station with an in-service date of June
2030

1
Page 43 of 301

Application Exhibit B

Page 2 of 15

b) Fort Churchill Additions 2 Project – 401 MW new simple-cycle aeroderivative gas
turbines at the Fort Churchill Generation Station with an in-service date of June
2031
c) Fort Churchill Additions 3 Project – 421 MW new simple-cycle frame gas turbines
at the Fort Churchill Generation Station with an in-service date of June 2032
•

Transmission projects associated with the renewable, storage, and thermal projects and
required to meet customers’ needs as presented in the Transmission Plan.

The Demand Side Management (“DSM”) programs proposed with this IRP provide grid value
with new savings targets and utilization of evolving planning processes that seek to create
foundational strategies to enable flexible load. Specifically, the proposed DSM Plan portfolio
implements a demand savings target reduction of 171 incremental new dispatchable MW with an
energy savings target of 687,755,000 kWh for the three-year period. The DSM Plan portfolio also
allocates at least 10 percent of its total Action Plan budget on programs directed to low-income
customers or those residing in historically underserved communities.
The 2026 Joint IRP also presents a Distributed Resources Plan (“DRP”) that contains a
Transportation Electrification Plan (“TEP”). The DRP meets the statutory and regulatory
objectives and continues to evolve as the Companies, the Commission and stakeholders become
more familiar with the technologies.
A complete list of all Action Plan items follows in Section II.

2026 JOINT ENERGY SUPPLY PLAN — ESP
Power Procurement Plan. Based on the 2026 ESP forecasts, Nevada Power has open power
positions in 2027 and 2028. The Companies propose to close the respective anticipated 2027 and
2028 open positions with firm products.
The Companies propose to continue the four-season laddering strategy to fill the remaining open
positions in 2027 and 2028 and begin filling subsequent years’ open positions. This plan is
consistent with the laddering strategy for closing the open power position, which was most recently
approved in Docket No. 25-08027. The power procurement laddering strategy will be executed in
coordination with the physical gas procurement plan in 2027-2029 with the procurement of
physical power and/or capacity acquired through a competitive bidding process. The Companies
propose to continue efforts to transact directly with counterparties as a supplement to the current
request for proposal process to seek non-standard firm energy products in an effort to address
short-term supply challenges during the evening and overnight net demand peak period when solar
resources are largely unavailable, given that battery storage is energy-limited. Additional
information regarding the closing of the open positions in the power procurement plan is provided
in Section 4.C of the ESP narrative.

9
Page 51 of 301

Application Exhibit B

Page 10 of 15

Additionally, the Companies are committed to continuously monitor the portfolio and seek to make
short-term and forward purchases when economic or needed to serve native load. Any proposed
purchases of greater than three years in duration will be submitted to the Commission for approval
in accordance with NAC § § 704.9113 and 704.9512.
The Companies are obligated to monitor their renewable portfolios on a continuous basis to ensure
that sufficient renewable energy and PCs are maintained to comply with RPS and undertake costeffective opportunities to fill new needs that may arise.
Fuel Procurement Plan. The fuel procurement plan is made up of three components: (1) a physical
gas procurement plan, (2) a gas transportation plan, and (3) a gas hedging plan.
1) Physical Gas Procurement Plan. The Companies plan to implement the four-season
laddering strategy originally approved by the Commission in Docket No. 09-09001 to
procure physical gas. Previously, the procurement of physical gas was subject to a cap on
the premium, which could be exceeded with prior approval from the Companies’ Risk
Committee. Consistent with the Stipulation in Docket No. 09-09001, if the Companies
exceeded the premium cap, and the procured gas that exceeded the premium cap was not
the least cost supply alternative, they would provide written notice to the Regulatory
Operations Staff of the Commission (“Staff”) and the Bureau of Consumer Protection
(“BCP”). The physical premium cap was originally approved in 2009 and has not been
adjusted for inflation. The Companies propose that the physical premium cap be removed.
2) Gas Transportation Plan. Nevada Power is connected directly to the interstate pipeline
systems with several major gas producing regions including the Permian, San Juan, and
the Rocky Mountain supply basins, as well as California gas supply. The largest producing
region with the best connectivity into and through Nevada Power’s control area is the
Rocky Mountain supply basin. The Kern River pipeline connects the Rocky Mountain
basin through Nevada into southern California with a design capacity of 2,166,575 million
British thermal units (“MMBtu”) per day. This pipeline deliverability capacity is large in
comparison to Nevada Power’s daily needs.
Sierra takes delivery of natural gas from three interstate pipelines, Great Basin, Tuscarora,
and Ruby Pipeline (Ruby). Great Basin receives gas supplies upstream and downstream
from Northwest Pipeline, which sources its gas supplies from British Columbia, the San
Juan Basin, and the Rocky Mountain region of Wyoming, Utah and Colorado. Tuscarora
receives gas supplies from GTN, near Malin, Oregon, which is connected to the gas
producing regions of Western Canada Sedimentary Basin Alberta through TC Energy’s
pipeline system. The gas supply source for Malin gas is predominantly in the Province of
Alberta, Canada. TC Energy’s Alberta pipeline system carries the gas commodity from the
10
Page 52 of 301

Application Exhibit B

Page 11 of 15

AECO producing areas of Canada to the Alberta/British Columbia border. There, TC
Energy’s Alberta system interconnects with TC Energy’s Foothills system, which
transports gas to TC Energy’s GTN system at the U.S./Canadian border near Kingsgate,
Idaho. GTN interconnects with Tuscarora near Malin. Sierra also takes natural gas delivery
from Ruby to supply Valmy. The gas supply source for Ruby is the Rocky Mountain.
The Companies are seeking approval to maintain their current natural gas transportation
portfolios. For Nevada Power, this requires authority to maintain seven existing gas
transportation contracts with Kern River Pipeline and three with Southwest Gas
Corporation. At Sierra, this requires authority to maintain a total of 37 existing gas
transportation and storage contracts with TC Energy – Alberta, TC Energy – Foothills, TC
Energy Gas Transmission Northwest (“GTN”), TC Energy Tuscarora Gas Transmission
Company (“Tuscarora”), Great Basin Gas Transmission Company (“Great Basin”),
Northwest Pipeline LLC (“NWPL”), Ruby Pipeline, and Pinyon Pipeline pursuant to rights
of first refusal and evergreen rights. The total projected annual costs for firm transportation
contracts at both Nevada Power and Sierra are approximately $128.1 million.
The contracts are listed in Figures ESP-43 and ESP-44. Additional information regarding
the Companies’ gas transportation plan is provided in Section 5.B of the ESP narrative.
The Companies seek acceptance and approval of the need for additional firm interstate
natural gas transportation capacity to support the Companies’ Preferred Plan. 1
3) Gas Hedging Plan. The Companies are proposing to continue the current approved
hedging strategy and acquire no natural gas hedges covering the ESP Action Plan period.
The Companies will continue to monitor the natural gas market fundamentals and
recommend changes to the hedging strategy in a future ESP update or ESP amendment as
necessary. The Companies will also continue bi-annual workshops with Staff and BCP to
review implementation for the approved no-hedge gas hedging strategy.
Risk Management Strategy. The Companies’ risk management strategy includes:
• Detailed corporate governance and risk control policies and procedures,
• Compliance with approved supply plans,
• Reduced reliance on volatile wholesale markets,
• Use of competitive procurement processes,
• Gas hedging strategies, and
• Market monitoring.
For more detail on risk management strategy, see Section 6 of the ESP narrative.
The ESP Gas Transportation Plan discusses a gas pipeline expansion for the three Fort Churchill Additions projects
requested under the Preferred Plan.

Application Exhibit B

Page 12 of 15

Determination of Prudence. Pursuant to NAC §§ 704.9508(2) and 704.9494, the Commission can
determine that the elements of an ESP are prudent if:
• The ESP balances the objectives of minimizing the cost of supply, minimizing retail price
volatility, and maximizing the reliability of supply over the term of the plan.
• The ESP optimizes the value of the overall supply portfolio of the utility for the benefit of
its bundled retail customers.
• The ESP does not contain any feature or mechanism that the Commission finds would
impair the restoration of the creditworthiness of the utility or would lead to a deterioration
of the creditworthiness of the utility.
This 2026 ESP satisfies the prudency requirements of NAC §§ 704.9508(2) and 704.9494 for each
of the three elements, as discussed in detail in Section 7 of the ESP narrative. The Companies
acknowledge that the prudence of their implementation of an approved 2026 ESP will be
determined in a future deferred energy proceeding. In addition, pursuant to NAC § 704.9504, the
Companies may deviate from an approved ESP or ESP update “to the extent necessary to respond
adequately to any significant change in circumstances not contemplated by the energy supply
plan.”
Continue to Conduct Gas Hedging Workshops. The Companies continue to conduct workshops
bi-annually with the Staff and BCP and provide updates in the form of presentations for the
remaining two quarters. Several topics are addressed, including energy market fundamentals, a
monitoring matrix for potential gas hedging strategies, forward sales activity, gas procurement,
and the most recent management decision on hedging.
SECTION III
FORECASTING DATA -— NAC § 704.9489(1)(c)
The Companies will continue to pursue improvements to its forecast models including economic
and price projections for all customer classes, end-use saturations, and efficiency trends.
SECTION IV
TIMETABLE AND BUDGET FOR PROGRAMS — NAC § 704.9489(1)(d), (3), (4)
The Action Plan timetable and budget by year is presented below. Further details regarding the
project schedules and milestones for capital projects are set forth in the Supply Plan. Additional
information regarding the Demand Side Management Plan budget can be found in the Demand
Side volume. Further details regarding the Distributed Resources Plan budget can be found in the
Distributed Resources Plan volume.

SECTION V
CHANGES IN METHODOLOGY — NAC § 704.9489(1)(e)
While some modeling techniques have been improved, especially in the areas of load forecasting
and DSM planning, and updates are described in the economic analysis narrative, the Companies
are not proposing any changes in basic planning methodologies.
SECTION VI
ACQUISITION OF NEW MODELING INSTRUMENTS — NAC § 704.9489(l)(f)
In Section 7.C. of the Distributed Resources Plan, the Companies discuss the DER Analytics
Toolset and Potential Study that introduces the new modeling instruments, DSMore and
LoadSEERTM, their purpose and utilization within this IRP, and planned future use. While some
other modeling techniques have been improved, the Companies are not proposing any other new
modeling instruments.
SECTION VII
DEMAND SIDE PLAN PROGRAMS — NAC § 704.9489(1)(g)
A description of continued planning efforts and the plan to carry out and continue selected
conservation and DSM measures is set forth in Section II. The Companies have not attempted to
claim or calculate imputed debt associated with energy efficiency contracts in the Preferred Plan.
SECTION VIII
ACQUISITION OF RESOURCES — NAC § 704.9489(1)(h)
During the Action Plan period, the Companies plan to enter into 23 PPAs for renewable and storage
resources totaling 4,550 MW of new renewable projects and 5,405 MW of new paired and
standalone BESS projects and construct three natural gas combustion turbine projects totaling
1,223 MW at the Fort Churchill Generating Station.
SECTION IX
ASSET RETIREMENT PLAN — NAC 704.9489(6)
Nevada Power and Sierra hold ownership interests in two generation assets that meet the criteria
of NAC § 704.9489(5), specifically:
•

2

Mohave Generating Station – Nevada Power: The Mohave site is in Laughlin, Nevada, and
is the previous site of a 1,500 MW coal-fired generating plant. Nevada Power is a 14
percent owner in the project. Southern California Edison (majority owner and operator)
and Los Angeles Department of Water and Power (who has assumed Salt River Project’s
original ownership share) are the other project owners. Mohave ceased operations January
1, 2006, and has been decommissioned. 2 In 2015, the co-owners agreed to proceed with

As defined in NRS § 704.7332.

14
Page 56 of 301

Application Exhibit B

Page 15 of 15

selling most of the property through a public sale process. The project has been marketed
for sale since 2016. If, and when, a transaction occurs and it is material, the net gains would
be included in a future general rate case for the benefit of Nevada Power’s customers. The
owners will continue to retain approximately 500 acres of land and post-closure care
associated with the closed onsite landfill.
•

Navajo Generating Station – Nevada Power: The Navajo Generating Station is located near
Page, Arizona, and is a previous site of a 2,250 MW total net capacity coal-fired facility.
Nevada Power is a 11.3 percent participant in the Navajo Generating Station. Arizona
Public Service, Los Angeles Department of Water and Power, Salt River Project, and
Tucson Electric Power are other participants in the plant, with Salt River Project also
holding an interest in Navajo on behalf of the United States. The coal-fired facility ceased
operation in November 2019. Site decommissioning, demolition, pond, and landfill closure
activities were completed in 2023. Site monitoring, post-closure care and groundwater
monitoring activities, and extension lease payments will continue through the site
remediation period ending December 22, 2054.

3(b)

A hosting capacity analysis of the distribution system. The hosting capacity analysis

NAC 704.9238

1

Notwithstanding the approval of the Commission of the distributed resources plan
of a utility, the utility may deviate from the approved distributed resources plan to
the extent necessary to respond adequately to any significant change in
circumstances not contemplated by the distributed resources plan. A significant
change in circumstances includes, without limitation:

No requirement in this filing

NAC 704.9238

1(a)

A material change in net system, feeder or nodal customer load or demand;

No requirement in this filing

NAC 704.9238

for any or all resources analyzed in the grid needs assessment;

No requirement in this filing

NAC 704.9238

1(c)

A material difference between estimated and actual in-service dates or performance
of the distributed resources analyzed and selected pursuant to the distributed
resources plan; and

No requirement in this filing

NAC 704.9238

1(d)

Any other circumstance that the utility demonstrates to the Commission warrants a
deviation.

No requirement in this filing

NAC 704.9238

2

NAC 704.934

DSM Narrative Sections 3 and 6-9

DSM Narrative Sections 3-4 and 6-9

NAC 704.934

Commission.

8

A utility shall include in its demand side plan an energy efficiency program for
residential customers which reduces the consumption of electricity or any fossil fuel.
The energy efficiency program must include, without limitation, the use of new
solar thermal energy sources.
The demand side plan must provide a list of the programs for which the utility is
requesting the approval of the Commission. The list must include, without
limitation:
An estimate of the reduction in the peak demand and energy consumption that
would result from each proposed program, in kilowatt-hours and kilowatts saved.
The programs must be listed according to their expected savings and their
contribution to a reduction in peak demand and energy consumption based upon
realistic estimates of the penetration of the market and the average life of the
programs.
An assessment of the costs of each proposed program and the savings produced by
the program. If the program can be relied upon to reduce peak demand on a firm
basis, the assessment must include the savings in the costs of transmission and
distribution.

The utility shall include with its demand side plan a report on the status of all
programs for energy efficiency and conservation that have been approved by the
Commission. The report must include tables for each such program showing, for
each year, the planned and achieved reduction in kilowatt-hours, the reduction in
kilowatts and the cost of the program.

conservation programs in the demand side plan must be directed to energy
efficiency and convservtion programs for low-income customers of the electric
utility

NAC 704.9357 Analysis of net economic benefits to State. (NRS 703.025,
704.210, 704.741)

NAC 704.9357

1

An analysis of the changes that result in net economic benefits to Nevada from
electricity-producing or electricity-saving resources must be conducted by the utility
in selecting a resource option. The net economic benefit to the State must be
quantified to reflect both the positive and negative changes and must include the net
economic impact of renewable resources. The projected present worth of societal
cost of a competing resource plan must be within 10 percent of the lowest societal
costs plan before proceeding with an analysis of the economic benefits to Nevada.

NAC 704.9359

NAC 704.9359 Determination of environmental costs to State. (NRS 703.025,
704.210, 704.741) The environmental costs to the State associated with operating
and maintaining a supply plan or demand side plan must be quantified for air
emissions, water and land use and the social cost of carbon as calculated pursuant to
subsection 5 of NAC 704.937. Environmental costs are those costs, wherever they
may occur, that result from harm or risks of harm to the environment after the
application of all mitigation measures required by existing environmental regulation
or otherwise included in the resource plan.

Summaries)

4

The utility shall calculate the present worth of societal costs for each alternative plan
for the supply of power. The present worth of societal costs of a particular
alternative plan must be determined by adding the environmental costs that are not
internalized as private costs to the utility pursuant to subsection 3 to the present
worth of future requirements for revenue. In calculating the present worth of
societal costs for each alternative plan pursuant to this subsection, the utility shall
include as environmental costs the utility’s estimate of the level of environmental
costs resulting from carbon dioxide emissions for that year and the social cost of
carbon.

Supply Plan Narrative, Section 3.G
(Environmental Externalities and Net
Economic Benefits);
Technical Appendix ECON-9
(NERA Report)

NAC 704.937

NAC 704.937

NAC 704.937

Page 66 of 301

Application Exhibit C
Page 9 of 30

NAC 704.937

5

For the purposes of subsection 4 and NAC 704.9215 and 704.9359, the social cost
of carbon must be determined by subtracting the costs associated with emissions of
carbon internalized as private costs to the utility pursuant to subsection 3 from the
net present value of the future global economic costs resulting from the emission of
each additional metric ton of carbon dioxide. The net present value of the future
global economic costs resulting from the emission of an additional ton of carbon
dioxide must be calculated using the best available science and economics such as
the analysis set forth in the “Technical Support Document: Technical Update of the
Social Cost of Carbon for Regulatory Impact Analysis” released by the Interagency
Working Group on Social Cost of Greenhouse Gases in August 2016. This
publication may be obtained, free of charge, at the Internet website
https://obamawhitehouse.archives.gov/sites/default/files/omb/inforeg/scc_tsd_final_
clean_8_26_16.pdf. The utility shall submit information supporting the method used
by the utility to calculate the social cost of carbon.

NAC 704.9395

In addition to the transmission plan requred by subsection 3, the supply plan of a
utility must include, as a discrete but integrated item in the supply plan, a
conceptual renewable energy zone transmission plan for the 20 years covered by the
forecast in the supply plan. The renewable energy zone transmission plan must
include distinct conceptual transmission plans, which may include capacity for
export to other states, for serving each of the renewable energy zones designated by
the Commission pursuant to NAC 704.880. Each of the distinct conceptual
transmission plans must include:
A description of the construction or expansion of transmission facilities required to
be added to the utility’s existing transmission system;
An estimate of cost at the planning level, including, without limitation, estimates for
permitting and other expenses of transmission development and estimated
development schedules for the transmission facilities included in the transmission
plan, based on information known by the utility at the time the transmission plan is
submitted to the Commission;
A description of any restrictions or limitations on the construction or expansion of
transmission facilities, including, without limitation, generator tie-lines in the
applicable transmission plan due to any local topographical, environmental,
governmental, land use or other factors or limitations that are known by the utility at
the time the transmission plan is submitted to the Commission; and
An estimate of the capacity of the renewable energy resources capable of being
developed in the applicable zone, based on information that is known to the utility at
the time the transmission plan is submitted to the Commission.
The supply plan of a utility must include the list of all assets of the utility required
by NRS 704.7338. If a utility owns only part of an asset included on the list, the
identity of every other owner and the percentage of the asset owned by each owner
must be set forth on the list.

NAC 704.9489 Requirements for action plan. (NRS 703.025, 704.210,
704.7339, 704.734, 704.741)
Each resource plan of a utility must include a detailed action plan based on an
integrated analysis of the demand side plan and supply plan of the utility. In its
action plan, the utility shall specify all its actions that are to take place during the 3
years commencing with the year following the year in which the resource plan is
filed. The action plan must contain:

NAC 704.9492 Rates for long-term avoided cost: Inclusion of certain
information in resource plan; estimation; specification of proposed limits
concerning availability. (NRS 703.025, 704.210, 704.741)

NAC 704.9492

NAC 704.9492

1

NAC 704.9492

2

NAC 704.9492

2(a)

NAC 704.9492

2(b)

A utility shall file, as part of its resource plan, the methodology for estimating the
rates for long-term avoided cost of the utility, including the capacity and energy
components. The rates for long-term avoided cost must be based upon the utility's
preferred plan and be consistent with 18 C.F.R. § 292.304(a), (b), (c) and (e).

Supply Plan Volume, Section 3.K
(Long-Term Avoided Costs)

The estimated rate for long-term avoided cost must be established for various sizes
of megawatt blocks, except that:
If the utility has a peak demand of at least 1,000 megawatts, the stated blocks must
not exceed 100 megawatts; and
If the utility has a peak demand of less than 1,000 megawatts, the stated blocks
must not exceed 10 percent of the system peak.

Supply Plan Volume, Section 3.K
(Long-Term Avoided Costs)
Supply Plan Volume, Section 3.K
(Long-Term Avoided Costs)
Supply Plan Volume, Section 3.K
(Long-Term Avoided Costs)

Supply Plan Volume, Section 3.K
(Long-Term Avoided Costs)

Supply Plan Volume, Section 3.K
(Long-Term Avoided Costs)

NAC 704.9492

3

The components for estimated long-term avoided cost capacity and energy rate must
be stated on a cents per kilowatt-hour basis for daily and seasonal peak and off-peak
periods and in such a manner that rates for various contract periods may be
calculated. At a minimum, the utility shall provide estimated rates for long-term
avoided cost for a 20-year contract and the long-term avoided cost by year for 5
years commencing in the year following the filing of the resource plan.

NAC 704.9492

4

In developing the estimated rates for long-term avoided cost, the proposed rates
must not be applied to renewable energy or to energy that is subject to the qualified
energy recovery process as defined in NRS 704.7809.

NAC 704.9492

5

NAC 704.9492

6

NAC 704.9492

7

The utility shall specify its proposed limits concerning the availability of the rates for
long-term avoided cost.
The resource plan of the utility must include the analyses and calculations used to
determine the proposed rates.
The selections of new distributed resources set forth in the distributed resources
plan must be reasonable.

DRP Narrative

Approval or modification of action plan; determination that elements of
energy supply plan and distributed resources plan are prudent; recovery of
costs to carry out approved plans.
(NRS 703.025, 704.210, 704.741, 704.751, 704.785)

NAC 704.9494
NAC 704.9494
NAC 704.9494

1
1(a)

The Commission will issue an order:
Approving the action plan of the utility as filed;

Not applicable at time of filing.

NAC 704.9494

1(b)

Modifying the action plan of the utility; or

Not applicable at time of filing.

NAC 704.9494

1(c)

If the plan is not approved as filed or modified, specifying those parts of the action
plan the Commission considers inadequate.

Not applicable at time of filing.

2

An action plan shall be deemed to be approved by the Commission only as to that
portion of the plan accepted as filed or modified with the consent of the utility
pursuant to subsection 1 of NRS 704.751.

Not applicable at time of filing.

3

Approval by the Commission of an action plan constitutes a finding that the
programs and projects contained in that action plan, other than the energy supply
plan and distributed resources plan, are prudent, including, without limitation,
construction of facilities, purchased power obligations, programs for energy
efficiency and conservation and impacts of imputed debt calculations associated
with renewable energy contracts or energy efficiency contracts. If the Commission
subsequently determines that any information relied upon when issuing its order
approving or modifying the action plan was based upon information that was known
or should have been known by the utility to be untrue or false at the time the
information was presented, the Commission may revoke, rescind or otherwise
modify its approval of the action plan.

Estimated rates for long-term avoided cost: General requirements; action by
Commission; solicitation of proposals; report. (NRS
703.025, 704.210, 704.741)
In conjunction with the issuance by the Commission of a final order approving or
modifying the action plan, the Commission will issue an order addressing the
utility's proposed estimated rates for long-term avoided cost, including the
methodology and limits to be used by the utility for its filing pursuant to NAC
704.9492. The Commission will consider the factors listed in 18 C.F.R. §
292.304(a), (b), (c) and (e) in its evaluation of the utility's proposed estimated rates
for long-term avoided cost.
The utility shall file with the Commission the utility's estimated rates for long-term
avoided cost within 60 days after the Commission issues its order pursuant to
subsection 1 specifying the methodology for estimating the rates for long-term
avoided cost.
The estimated rates for long-term avoided cost filed by the utility with the
Commission pursuant to subsection 2 must:
Be consistent with the methodology for estimating the long-term avoided cost
approved by the Commission and be based upon the resource plan approved by the
Commission;
Unless otherwise ordered by the Commission, be consistent with the format set
forth in subsections 2 and 3 of NAC 704.9492 and be limited to those rates
proposed by the utility pursuant to subsection 5 of NAC 704.9492.
If required, the Commission will hold a hearing on the estimated rates for long-term
avoided cost within 90 days after the utility files the estimated rates for long-term
avoided cost pursuant to subsection 2. If a hearing is held, the Commission will
issue an order on the matter within 45 days after the conclusion of the hearing.
Within 30 days after the date on which the Commission issues an order pursuant to
subsection 4, the utility shall solicit proposals to provide the utility capacity or
energy, or both, in a manner that complies with the methodology for estimating longterm avoided cost approved by the Commission.
Within 90 days after issuing a solicitation of proposals pursuant to subsection 5, the
utility shall file with the Commission a report concerning the results of the
solicitation
The utility’s rate for long-term avoided cost for each block must be the estimated
rate for long-term avoided cost established pursuant to this section or the
competitive rate solicited pursuant to subsection 5, whichever is lower.

NAC 704.95225 Recovery of certain amounts based on measurable and

NAC 704.95225

NAC 704.95225

NAC 704.95225

2

An electric utility may recover an amount based on the measurable and verifiable
effects of the implementation by the electric utility of programs for energy efficiency
and conservation described in the demand side plan of the electric utility and
approved by the Commission pursuant to NAC 704.9494 as part of the action plan
of the electric utility. The amount recovered must include:
The costs reasonably incurred by the electric utility in implementing and
administering the programs for energy efficiency and conservation, which are
recovered pursuant to paragraph (a) of subsection 2 of NAC 704.9523; and
An amount equal to the costs reasonably incurred by the electric utility in
implementing and administering the programs for energy efficiency and
conservation multiplied by the electric utility’s authorized overall rate of return
grossed up for taxes applicable to the utility’s equity portion of the authorized rate
of return, which is recovered pursuant to paragraph (b) of subsection 2 of NAC
704 9523
The Commission will consider the effect of any recovery pursuant to this section on
the rates of the customers of the electric utility.

NRS 704.741

NRS 704.741 Plan to increase supply or decrease demands: Submission
not less than triennially; joint plans by certain affiliated utilities;
contents prescribed by regulation; requirements.

NRS 704.741

1

A utility which supplies electricity in this State shall, on or before June 1 of
every third year, or more often if necessary, in the manner specified by the
Commission, submit a plan to increase its supply of electricity or decrease
the demands made on its system by its customers to the Commission. Two or
more utilities that are affiliated through common ownership and that have an
interconnected system for the transmission of electricity shall submit a joint
plan.

NRS 704.741

methods or formulas which are used by the utility or utilities to:

demands of the utility or utilities must not include the amount of energy and
capacity proposed pursuant to subsection 6 as annual limits on the total
amount of energy and capacity that eligible customers may be authorized to
purchase from providers of new electric resources through transactions
approved by the Commission pursuant to an application submitted pursuant
to NRS 704B.310 on or after May 16, 2019; and

PUBLIC UTILITIES COMMISSION OF NEVADA
DRAFT NOTICE
(Applications, Tariff Filings, Complaints, and Petitions)
Pursuant to Nevada Administrative Code (“NAC”) 703.162, the Commission requires that a draft
notice be included with all applications, tariff filings, complaints and petitions. Please complete and
include ONE COPY of this form with your filing. (Completion of this form may require the use of
more than one page.)
A title that generally describes the relief requested (see NAC 703.160(5)(a)):
Joint Application of Nevada Power Company d/b/a NV Energy and Sierra
Pacific Power Company d/b/a NV Energy for approval of their 2027-2046
Integrated Resource Plan, 2027-2029 Action Plan and 2027-2029 Energy
Supply Plan.
The name of the applicant, complainant, petitioner or the name of the agent for the applicant,
complainant or petitioner (see NAC 703.160(5)(b)):
Nevada Power Company d/b/a NV Energy and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy.
A brief description of the purpose of the filing or proceeding, including, without limitation, a clear
and concise introductory statement that summarizes the relief requested or the type of proceeding
scheduled (see NAC 703.160(5)(c)):
Nevada Power Company and Sierra Pacific Power Company are seeking
approval of the 2026 Joint Integrated Resource Plan (“IRP”). The 2026 Joint
IRP presents analysis of resource options (energy efficiency and conservation,
distributed energy resources, renewable generation, thermal generation and
transmission) available to the Companies to meet their bundled retail or native
load over 20 years. Embodied in this 2026 Joint IRP is an updated load
forecast, a market fundamentals forecast including fuel and purchase power
pricing, a supply plan that includes transmission, generation and renewable
resources, an economic analysis and, finally, a financial plan. Embedded in the
2026 Joint IRP is a Joint Energy Supply Plan (“ESP”), as required by Nevada
Administrative Code (“NAC”) § 704.9482. The Joint ESP and its associated
technical appendices are located in stand-alone volumes filed as part of the
2026 Joint IRP. The 2026 Joint IRP also includes a demand side management
(“DSM”) plan and a distributed resources plan (“DRP”). The 2026 Joint IRP
addresses a step-change load growth driven by data centers by seeking
approval of 23 third-party power purchase agreements, adding approximately
4,550 megawatts of renewable generation and 5,405 megawatts of battery
storage, as well as 1,223 megawatts of thermal generation and related
transmission facilities.

No. A consumer session is not required by NRS § 704.069.

number(s) or schedule number(s) being revised.

1 NRS 704.069 states in pertinent part:

1. The Commission shall conduct a consumer session to solicit comments from the public in any matter pending before the
Commission pursuant to NRS 704.061 to 704.110 inclusive, in which:
(a) A public utility has filed a general rate application, an application to recover the increased cost of purchased fuel,
purchased power, or natural gas purchased for resale or an application to clear its deferred accounts; and
(b) The changes proposed in the application will result in an increase in annual gross operating revenue, as certified by the
applicant, in an amount that will exceed $50,000 or 10 percent of the applicant’s annual gross operating revenue, whichever
is less.

9 I.

RESOURCE PLANNING PROCESS

10
11 7.

Q.

PLEASE DESCRIBE THE PREPARATION OF THE 2026 JOINT IRP.

12

A.

Although Nevada law generally contemplates the Companies file a joint IRP every

13

three years under NRS 704.741, the statute allows for more frequent IRP filings as

14

necessary. The Companies are filing the 2026 Joint IRP at this time because they

15

are at a pivotal time in planning for unprecedented, step-change load growth driven

16

by large customer data center and artificial intelligence (“AI”) related service

17

requests. Given the scope of this filing, it is helpful to understand the analytical

18

steps the Companies use to develop and evaluate candidate resource portfolios in

19

the IRP process.

20
21

To aid in understanding the economic analysis performed for the 2026 Joint IRP,

22

an overall flowchart of the process is provided in the Economic Analysis section of

23

the Narrative. At a high level, the IRP process starts with a forecast of customer

24

loads and assesses a range of supply alternatives to identify options for meeting

25

Nevada’s capacity and energy needs. The Resource Planning team uses PLEXOS

26

modeling tools, along with supporting analyses, to determine a set of least-cost,

27
28 Wells-DIRECT

6
Page 98 of 301

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

1

reasonable, and reliable resource portfolios that meet applicable requirements,

2

including resource adequacy and renewable policy objectives. After performing

3

that analysis, the results are assessed, alternative plans are selected, and ultimately

4

a preferred plan and alternate plan are identified. As discussed in more detail in the

5

testimony of Kimberly Williams and other expert witnesses, this filing includes

6

updates to key inputs and assumptions, including the load forecast, market

7

fundamentals and fuel and purchased power price forecasts, federal climate policy

8

scenarios, and the Companies’ reliability and deliverability-focused screening and

9

evaluation considerations. Through this filing, the Companies ask the Commission

10

to determine that the Preferred Plan the Companies have selected is reasonable and

11

prudent and to authorize the Companies to take all actions necessary during the

12

three-year action plan period, 2027–2029, to implement the Preferred Plan, subject

13

to the specific approvals requested in this docket and in subsequent filings as

14

required.

15
16

Embodied in this 2026 Joint IRP is an updated load forecast, a market fundamentals

17

forecast including fuel and purchased power pricing, a Supply Plan that includes

18

Transmission, Renewables, and Generation components, the Economic Analysis,

19

and the Financial Plan. The 2026 Joint IRP is filed together with a Joint Energy

20

Supply Plan (“ESP”), as required by Nevada Administrative Code (“NAC”) §

21

704.9482, with its associated technical appendices filed as stand-alone volumes.

22

The 2026 Joint IRP also includes a demand side management (“DSM”) plan and a

23

distributed resource plan (“DRP”). The 2026 Joint IRP addresses capacity and

24

renewable portfolio standard (“RPS”) challenges created by step-change load

25

growth, evolving federal and state policy signals, and deliverability constraints,

26

while continuing to focus on resource adequacy.

27

Q.

4

A.

After evaluating five alternative plans, the Companies identified the Obsidian Plan

5

as the Preferred Plan for the 2026 Joint IRP. The Obsidian Plan reflects a

6

deliverability- and flexibility-focused approach to addressing unprecedented, step-

7

change load growth while maintaining reliability and continuing progress toward

8

Nevada’s clean energy objectives. Consistent with the Supply Plan, Economic

9

Analysis and supporting testimony, the Preferred Plan includes a balanced mix of

10

(i) renewable and battery storage resources, (ii) firm capacity resources to support

11

resource adequacy and operational flexibility, and (iii) transmission and

12

interconnection-related infrastructure to support reliable delivery and integration of

13

new resources and address customer needs. The Companies’ specific requests for

14

Commission approval of near-term resources within the action plan period are

15

described in the Supply Plan and sponsored by the Companies’ subject-matter

16

witnesses.

PREFERRED PLAN.

PLEASE DESCRIBE HOW THE COMPANIES SELECTED THE

A.

The Companies selected the Preferred Plan after evaluating five alternative plans

17

18

19

policy, consistent with NAC requirements and as described in the Economic

20

21

22

23

future filings. The Obsidian Plan was selected because it provides a balanced,

24

executable mix of renewable, storage, and firm capacity resources to reduce market

25

reliance and manage cost and execution risk relative to other plans. At the same

26

time, the Obsidian Plan maintains the flexibility to pivot as the load develops,

Q.

IN THE COMPANIES’ PLANNING AND DECISION MAKING?

5
6

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

WHY DOES RESOURCE ADEQUACY REMAIN A CRITICAL FACTOR

A.

Resource adequacy remains critical in the 2026 Joint IRP because unprecedented

7

load growth is occurring amid supply constraints and uncertainty regarding the

8

availability and deliverability of market capacity and energy. As reflected in the

9

Economic Analysis and supported by Mr. Holland, the Companies are focused on

10

reducing the open position—the portion of capacity needs not met by company-

11

owned resources or long-term contracts—to limit reliance on the external market

12

and support reliable service.

13
14

The Companies also evaluated near-term Sierra subsystem resource adequacy

15

given the concentration and timing of load growth prior to commercial operation of

16

the Greenlink Nevada Transmission Project (modeled to be fully in service by the

17

beginning of 2029). 2

18
19

These considerations reinforce the Companies’ emphasis on adding deliverable

20

capacity within the Companies’ balancing authority area and reducing the open

21

position.

22
23
24
2

The Companies adopted a planning assumption that Greenlink West will be in service on December 31, 2027. This

25 assumption supports prudent reliability planning by reflecting the potential for schedule risk while the Companies

continue to pursue actions intended to achieve the May 31, 2027, target in-service date. However, the May 31, 2027,

26 in-service date remains at risk.
27
28 Wells-DIRECT

11
Page 103 of 301

1 12.

Q.

GREATER RISK?

2

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

3

DOES HAVING A LARGE OPEN POSITION PUT THE COMPANIES AT

A.

Yes. As described in the 2026 Joint IRP Economic Analysis, the Companies’ “open

4

position” represents the portion of the Companies’ capacity needs that are not met

5

by resources under the Companies ownership or long-term contract. A larger open

6

position increases exposure to the uncertain availability and deliverability of market

7

capacity and energy—particularly during stressed system conditions—thereby

8

increasing reliability risk. Accordingly, the Companies’ planning approach

9

emphasizes adding deliverable resources and contracts within the Companies’

10

balancing authority area and otherwise reducing market reliance to prudently

11

support peak capacity needs under the updated 2026 Joint IRP load outlook.

12
13 13.

Q.

THEIR OPEN POSITION DURING THE STUDY PERIOD?

14
15

DOES THE PREFERRED PLAN HELP THE COMPANIES REDUCE

A.

Yes. As shown in the Economic Analysis and the capacity-needs figures in this

16

testimony, the Companies’ open position is significant in 2027 under a portfolio

17

limited to existing and previously approved resources. Under the Preferred Plan,

18

the open position is reduced stepwise through the addition of deliverable resources,

19

and remains at or below 500 MW in 2031 and subsequent years with the help of

20

future placeholder resources as load growth continues.

21
22 14.

THE COMPANIES ARE REQUESTING APPROVAL OF FOSSIL

23

GENERATION AS A PART OF THE PREFERRED PLAN. ARE THE

24

COMPANIES DEVIATING FROM THEIR CLEAN ENERGY GOALS?

26

No. The Companies remain committed to Nevada’s sustainability objectives. As
described in the Renewables Section of the 2026 Joint IRP Supply Plan, the

27
28 Wells-DIRECT

4

across the Nevada Power and Sierra systems. At the same time, as described in the

5

2026 Joint IRP Economic Analysis, the Preferred Plan includes additions of near-

6

7

8

adequacy and operational flexibility as load grows and as the effective load carrying

9

10

11

than for sustained energy production, while the Preferred Plan continues to rely on

significant renewable and storage additions to serve energy needs and advance

13

carbon-reduction objectives.

Q.

DO THE COMPANIES NEED THE RENEWABLE RESOURCES IN THE

16

PREFERRED

17

REQUIREMENTS?

18

A.

PLAN

TO

MEET

THE

STATE-MANDATED

RPS

Yes. As discussed in the Renewables Section of the 2026 Joint IRP Supply Plan

19

and in the Renewable Outlook section of this testimony, the Companies need

20

substantial incremental renewable and storage resources to meet Nevada’s

21

escalating RPS requirements—42 percent in 2027 and 50 percent in 2030—and to

22

continue progress toward the State’s 2050 clean energy goal. Although the

23

Companies have exceeded the RPS requirement to date, the Companies’ RPS

24

outlook projects potential non-compliance in 2027 through 2032 in large part due

25

to the escalating RPS requirement coupled with projected retail load growth. At the

26

same time, bringing new renewable resources online is challenging due to federal

27
28 Wells-DIRECT

13
Page 105 of 301

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

1

policy surrounding certain renewable resource types, and resource cancellations.

2

The Companies therefore seek approval of a large set of renewable and storage

3

PPAs in this Docket, which would materially increase the Companies’ ability to

4

generate portfolio energy credits and address projected shortfalls. While the

5

Companies may use banked credits and credit sharing between Nevada Power and

6

Sierra as part of their compliance strategy, the Renewables Section of the Supply

7

Plan shows that the existing surplus credit bank is expected to decline significantly

8

under the updated load outlook, which limits the ability to rely on past surpluses as

9

a long-term solution.

11 16.

Q.

IF THE COMPANIES PROJECT THAT THE RPS WILL NOT BE

12

ACHIEVED UNTIL 2033, WHY DO THEY LIMIT RENEWABLE

13

PLACEHOLDER RESOURCES IN 2031 AND 2032 IN THE IRP PLANS?

14

A.

Recent federal policy developments and administrative actions have increased the

15

risk that otherwise economic wind and solar projects may experience delayed

16

development or reduced value due to permitting bottlenecks and uncertainty

17

regarding approvals that require federal sign-off. The Companies limit annual

18

placeholder renewable resources to no more than 200 MW of solar, 0 MW of wind

19

and 50 MW of geothermal in 2031 and 2032, determined via a stepwise analysis

20

described in the Economic Analysis Narrative, due to expectations that this federal

21

policy would result in limited availability of implementable solar and wind

22

resources in the near term, making reliance on these future placeholder resources,

23

in lieu of real projects, imprudent, while at the same time geothermal resources

24

have typically not been available in large quantities in the Companies’ requests for

25

proposals (“RFP”). These limitations were not applied in the Low Carbon Plan

26
27
28 Wells-DIRECT

14
Page 106 of 301

1

which requires an unrealistically accelerated very-near-term buildout of renewable

2

and storage resources.

4 17.

Q.

LOW CARBON PLAN, AND NO OPEN POSITION PLAN.

5

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

6

PLEASE DESCRIBE THE ALTERNATE PLAN, WILDHORSE PLAN,

A.

The 2026 Joint IRP identifies an Alternate Plan referred to as the Ward Plan. As

7

summarized in the Economic Analysis and testimony of Kimberly Williams, the

8

Ward Plan is substantially similar to the Preferred (Obsidian) Plan in overall

9

resource mix and objectives, but differs in certain resource selections and timing—

10

most notably, the amount of thermal capacity added in 2030 and the type of thermal

11

capacity added in 2031—while still relying on significant renewable and storage

12

additions and associated transmission to meet load growth and reliability needs.

13

The Economic Analysis also discusses an additional alternative plan referred to as

14

the Wildhorse Plan, in which more thermal capacity is added in 2031 relative to the

15

Preferred Plan. Consistent with the Commission’s guidance, the Economic

16

Analysis also includes “named placeholder” projects in the alternative plans to

17

represent reasonably known candidate projects; those placeholders are not

18

requested for approval in this filing.

19
20

The Low Carbon Plan is a required alternative under NAC § 704.937(1) and is

21

designed to achieve an 80 percent reduction in carbon dioxide emissions from 2005

22

levels by 2030. As reflected in the Economic Analysis, this constraint results in an

23

unrealistically accelerated very-near-term buildout of renewable and storage

24

resources relative to the Preferred Plan. While not viable, this plan illustrates the

25

tradeoffs in portfolio cost when pursuing a more stringent near-term emissions

26

trajectory.

27
28 Wells-DIRECT

15
Page 107 of 301

1

Pursuant to NRS § 704.741(3)(c)(2), the No Open Position Plan is designed to

2

close the Companies’ open position by 2032 and maintain that position thereafter.

3

Because it targets minimal reliance on market purchases for capacity, this plan

4

requires additional firm capacity and/or storage resources relative to the Preferred

5

Plan, and it illustrates the cost and execution implications of attempting to fully

6

eliminate the open position under the updated 2026 Joint IRP Base Load Forecast.

and Sierra Pacific Power Company

Q.

PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR SUPPORT OF THE PREFERRED PLAN.

18

A.

I support the Preferred (Obsidian) Plan because it responds to current circumstances

19

reflected in the 2026 Joint IRP, including step-change load growth, constrained and

20

uncertain regional market availability and deliverability, escalating RPS

21

requirements, and execution realities associated with permitting, interconnection,

22

and transmission timelines. The Preferred Plan also depends on large customer load

23

commitments under a Commission-approved LLESA framework to establish clear

24

rules for planning for the large load requests reflected in this IRP and in future IRPs.

25

Overall, the Obsidian Plan provides a balanced, deliverable portfolio that reduces

26

the Companies’ open position in the near term, advances significant renewable and

27
28 Wells-DIRECT

16
Page 108 of 301

1

storage additions, and includes firm dispatchable capacity to support resource

2

adequacy and operational flexibility as load grows and as renewable and storage

3

contributions change over time. The Obsidian Plan adds significant new generating

4

and storage projects to serve the growing load but also leaves room to pivot in future

5

filings as the load develops, technology advances, the economic environment

6

changes, and federal and state policy evolves.

8 III:

FORM LLESA

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

9
10 19.

Q.

PLEASE PROVIDE AN OVERALL EXPLANATION OF THE FORM

11

LARGE LOAD ELECTRIC SERVICE AGREEMENT AND ITS PURPOSE

12

IN THIS PROCEEDING.

13

A.

The Companies request Commission approval of a standard form Large Load

14

Electric Service Agreement (“LLESA”) for eligible large customers taking bundled

15

service under Schedule LGS-3 / GS-3. The LLESA begins with the bundled

16

Schedule LGS-3/GS-3 and adds rate components and contractual terms that are

17

intended to provide clear rules for serving very large new or accelerated load

18

requests while protecting existing customers through cost-causation-based

19

provisions. In general, the agreement ties the customer’s requested load levels and

20

ramp schedule to defined minimum-billing and security requirements and

21

conditions long-term supply commitments on Commission approval of the

22

underlying resources or other approved alternatives.

23
24
25
26
27
28 Wells-DIRECT

17
Page 109 of 301

1 20.

HOW DO THE KEY PROVISIONS OF THE LLESA CREATE CLEAR

2

REQUIREMENTS

3

The LLESA aligns responsibility with cost causation by pairing the Companies’

5

obligation to plan for and serve significant load with customer commitments that

6

help reduce stranded-investment risk and protect existing customers. Among other

7

8

9

Q.

LOAD ELECTRIC SERVICE AGREEMENT.

8

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

9

PLEASE DESCRIBE THE APPLICABILITY OF THE FORM LARGE

A.

The form LLESA is intended to apply to eligible large customers with at least 25

10

MW taking bundled retail electric service under Schedule LGS-3 / GS-3 whose

11

requested load size, ramp timing, or load volatility may require the Companies to

12

develop incremental resources beyond what would otherwise be necessary to serve

13

existing customers. The agreement is executed on a customer-specific basis to

14

reflect the customer’s ramp schedule and is expressly conditioned on Commission

15

approvals required to implement the agreement and, where applicable, to approve

16

or otherwise authorize related supply arrangements. The LLESA is designed to

17

operate in coordination with other required agreements and processes, such as Rule

18

9 interconnection and related facilities arrangements. In this manner, the LLESA

19

defines when and how the Companies will make long-term generation and energy

20

commitments for large load requests, while establishing minimum billing,

21

termination, and security provisions intended to protect existing customers from

22

cost shifts.

23
24
25
26
27
28 Wells-DIRECT

19
Page 111 of 301

1 23.

HOW DID THE COMPANIES DETERMINE WHEN THE FORM LLESA

2

IS APPLICABLE FOR NEW AND EXISTING ACCELERATED LARGE

3

CUSTOMER LOADS OF 25 MW OR GREATER?

4

Nevada Power Company
and Sierra Pacific Power Company
d/b/a NV Energy

Q.

A.

The Companies evaluated applicability based first on the load’s expected

5

characteristics including the customer’s anticipated load shape, magnitude, and

6

timing of the requested load. The Companies then consider the point at which

7

serving that load could reasonably require incremental procurement and/or

8

incremental generation and transmission investments beyond what would otherwise

9

be needed to serve existing customers. Given existing generation and related

10

transmission constraints, large load requests will require procurement or

11

investment.

12
13

In practice, the Companies first confirm that the customer is an eligible bundled

14

retail customer taking service under Schedule LGS-3 / GS-3 and that the request is

15

for a load of at least 25 MW (whether a new customer request or an increase that

16

accelerates an existing customer’s load above prior expectations). The Companies

17

then assess the customer’s requested in-service date(s), ramp schedule, and

18

expected load characteristics to determine whether the request represents a new or

19

accelerated large load addition, and whether the load’s size, timing, and anticipated

20

load shape with other similarly situated customers create a need for incremental

21

resource actions or increase stranded investment risk. Where those conditions are

22

present, the Companies apply the form LLESA to establish the customer’s

23

contractual commitments, including ramp and minimum-billing obligations and

24

associated security, so that incremental costs driven by the new or accelerated large

25

load are aligned with cost causation and are not shifted to existing customers.

26
27

24

25
